                        {src: '/oscar-static/js/shared-es5-bundle-e122e5f233.js', test: 'shared-js', nomodule: true},
                        {src: '/oscar-static/js/header-150-es6-bundle-8395667412.js', test: 'header-150-js', module: true},
                        {src: '/oscar-static/js/header-150-es5-bundle-95c03c3888.js', test: 'header-150-js', nomodule: true}
                    
                ].filter(function (s) {
                    if (s.src === null) return false;
                    if (moduleSupport && s.nomodule) return false;
                    return !(!moduleSupport && s.module);
                }),

                register: function (value) {
                    this.registered.push(value);
                },

                ready: function () {
                    if (this.registered.length === this.scripts.length) {
                        this.registered.forEach(function (fn) {
                            if (typeof fn === 'function') {
                                setTimeout(fn, 0); 
                            }
                        });
                        this.ready = function () {};
                    }
                },

                insert: function (s) {
                    var t = d.getElementById('js-position' + this.index);
                    if (t && t.insertAdjacentElement) {
                        t.insertAdjacentElement('afterend', s);
                    } else {
                        d.head.appendChild(s);
                    }
                    ++this.index;
                },

                createScript: function (script, beforeLoad) {
                    var s = d.createElement('script');
                    s.id = 'js-position' + (this.index + 1);
                    s.setAttribute('data-test', script.test);
                    if (beforeLoad) {
                        s.defer = 'defer';
                        s.onload = function () {
                            if (script.noinit) {
                                loader.register(true);
                            }
                            if (d.readyState === 'interactive' || d.readyState === 'complete') {
                                loader.ready();
                            }
                        };
                    } else {
                        s.async = 'async';
                    }
                    s.src = script.src;
                    return s;
                },

                init: function () {
                    this.scripts.forEach(function (s) {
                        loader.insert(loader.createScript(s, true));
                    });

                    d.addEventListener('DOMContentLoaded', function () {
                        loader.ready();
                        var conditionalScripts;
                        
                            conditionalScripts = [
                                {match: 'div[data-pan-container]', src: '/oscar-static/js/pan-zoom-es6-bundle-1a117d29d6.js', test: 'pan-zoom-js',  module: true },
                                {match: 'div[data-pan-container]', src: '/oscar-static/js/pan-zoom-es5-bundle-1a117d29d6.js', test: 'pan-zoom-js',  nomodule: true },
                                {match: 'math,span.mathjax-tex', src: '/oscar-static/js/math-es6-bundle-8da0cf81d9.js', test: 'math-js', module: true},
                                {match: 'math,span.mathjax-tex', src: '/oscar-static/js/math-es5-bundle-8da0cf81d9.js', test: 'math-js', nomodule: true}
                            ];
                        

                        if (conditionalScripts) {
                            conditionalScripts.filter(function (script) {
                                return !!document.querySelector(script.match) && !((moduleSupport && script.nomodule) || (!moduleSupport && script.module));
                            }).forEach(function (script) {
                                loader.insert(loader.createScript(script));
                            });
                        }
                    }, false);
                }
            };
            loader.init();
        }
    })(window, document);
</script>

Despite significant advances over the past decade, the developments in agricultural domain have faced challenges due to a scarcity of datasets. To facilitate 3D point cloud research in agriculture community, we introduce Crops3D, the diverse real-world dataset derived from authentic agricultural scenarios.     <meta name="dc.description" content="Point cloud analysis is a crucial task in computer vision. Despite significant advances over the past decade, the developments in agricultural domain have faced challenges due to a scarcity of datasets. To facilitate 3D point cloud research in agriculture community, we introduce Crops3D, the diverse real-world dataset derived from authentic agricultural scenarios. Crops3D distinguishes itself through its unique properties: diversity, authenticity, and complexity. The dataset incorporates data from diverse point cloud acquisition methods, encompassing eight distinct crop types with 1,230 samples, authentically representing crops in the real-world. It stands as the pioneering dataset that comprehensively supports the three critical tasks in 3D crop phenotyping: instance segmentation of individual plants in agricultural settings, plant type perception, and plant organ segmentation. Additionally, the intricate crop structures in Crops3D exhibit higher complexity than available 3D public datasets, showcasing substantial self-occlusion and increased complexity as crops mature. We analyse diverse crop point cloud acquisition methods and evaluate multiple models&#8217; performance with the Crops3D dataset."/>

Despite significant advances over the past decade, the developments in agricultural domain have faced challenges due to a scarcity of datasets. To facilitate 3D point cloud research in agriculture community, we introduce Crops3D, the diverse real-world dataset derived from authentic agricultural scenarios.                 <div class="c-article-body">
                    <section aria-labelledby="Abs1" data-title="Abstract" lang="en"><div class="c-article-section" id="Abs1-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Abs1">Abstract</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Abs1-content"><p>Point cloud analysis is a crucial task in computer vision. Despite significant advances over the past decade, the developments in agricultural domain have faced challenges due to a scarcity of datasets. To facilitate 3D point cloud research in agriculture community, we introduce Crops3D, the diverse real-world dataset derived from authentic agricultural scenarios. Crops3D distinguishes itself through its unique properties: diversity, authenticity, and complexity. The dataset incorporates data from diverse point cloud acquisition methods, encompassing eight distinct crop types with 1,230 samples, authentically representing crops in the real-world. It stands as the pioneering dataset that comprehensively supports the three critical tasks in 3D crop phenotyping: instance segmentation of individual plants in agricultural settings, plant type perception, and plant organ segmentation. Additionally, the intricate crop structures in Crops3D exhibit higher complexity than available 3D public datasets, showcasing substantial self-occlusion and increased complexity as crops mature. We analyse diverse crop point cloud acquisition methods and evaluate multiple models’ performance with the Crops3D dataset.</p></div></div></section>

                        <div class="main-content">
                            <section data-title="Background &amp; Summary"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec1-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec1">Background &amp; Summary</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec1-content"><p>Recent years have witnessed a substantial and noteworthy surge in research on exploring the applications of point clouds within agriculture<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 1" title="Luo, L. et al. Eff-3dpseg: 3d organ-level plant shoot segmentation using annotation-efficient deep learning. Plant Phenomics. 5, 0080, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.34133/plantphenomics.0080&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR1" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e621">1</a></sup>. This technology has found widespread utilization in various domains including phenotyping research<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 2" title="Li, Z., Guo, R., Li, M., Chen, Y. &amp; Li, G. A review of computer vision technologies for plant phenotyping. Comput. Electron. Agric. 176, 105672, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/ICCVW60793.2023.00077&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2020)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR2" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e625">2</a></sup>, agricultural robotics<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 3" title="Montes, H. A., Le Louedec, J., Cielniak, G. &amp; Duckett, T.Real-time detection of broccoli crops in 3D point clouds for autonomous robotic harvesting. In: Proceedings of the IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS). PP 10483–10488, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/IROS45743.2020.9341381&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2020)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR3" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e629">3</a></sup> and precision agriculture<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 4" title="Comba, L., Biglia, A., Aimonino, D. R. &amp; Gay, P. Unsupervised detection of vineyards by 3D point-cloud UAV photogrammetry for precision agriculture. Comput. Electron. Agric. 155, 84–95, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1016/j.compag.2018.10.005&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2018)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR4" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e633">4</a></sup>. Point clouds offer invaluable insights, enabling the monitoring of plant status, improving production efficiency, and predicting yields with precision<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 5" title="Villalpando, A. P., Kubisch, M., Colliaux, D., Hanappe, P. &amp; Hafner, V. V.Reinforcement learning with space carving for plant scanning. In: Proceedings of the IEEE/CVF International Conference on Computer Vision (ICCVW). PP 694–701, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/ICCVW60793.2023.00077&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR5" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e637">5</a></sup>.</p><p>In the initial phases of agricultural production and crop phenotyping research, gathering information regarding crop growth involved laborious manual observations and measurements, incurring high costs and time investments<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 6" title="Guo, Q. et al. Crop 3D—a LiDAR based platform for 3D high-throughput crop phenotyping. Sci. China Life Sci. 61, 328–339, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1007/s11427-017-9056-0&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2018)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR6" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e644">6</a></sup>. The advent of computer vision and image sensors introduced the use of 2D images for parameterized crop representations, significantly enhancing efficiency while reducing costs and enabling more objective assessments<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 7" title="Li, L., Zhang, Q. &amp; Huang, D. A review of imaging techniques for plant phenotyping. Sensors. 14, 20078–20111, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.3390/s141120078&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2014)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR7" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e648">7</a></sup>. However, despite these advantages, 2D images lack depth information and possess limitations in accurately representing the intricate 3D structures of crops. Conversely, point clouds, as a robust three-dimensional data representation, excel in capturing the complex morphology of crops<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 8" title="Lou, L. et al. Estimation of branch angle from 3D point cloud of plants. In: Proceedings of the International Conference on 3D Vision. PP 554–561, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/3DV.2015.68&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2015)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR8" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e652">8</a></sup>. In contrast to images, point clouds provide a detailed and intuitive portrayal of the intricate 3D structures of crops<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 9" title="Paproki, A., Sirault, X., Berry, S., Furbank, R. &amp; Fripp, J. A novel mesh processing based technique for 3D plant analysis. BMC Plant Biol. 12, 1–13, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2229-12-63&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2012)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR9" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e656">9</a></sup>. Consequently, their utilization is increasingly pivotal in agricultural contexts.</p><p>Point cloud data plays a crucial role in high-throughput crop phenotyping, especially when combined with deep learning techniques for automated perception and segmentation. This integration unlocks significant potential by enabling the efficient identification and separation of organs<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Li, Y. et al. Automatic organ-level point cloud segmentation of maize shoots by integrating high-throughput data acquisition and deep learning. Comput. Electron. Agric. 193, 106702, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1016/j.compag.2022.106702&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2022)." href="#ref-CR10" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e663">10</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Masuda, T.Leaf area estimation by semantic segmentation of point cloud of tomato plants. In: Proceedings of the IEEE/CVF International Conference on Computer Vision Workshops (ICCVW). PP 1381–1389, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/ICCVW54120.2021.00159&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2021)." href="#ref-CR11" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e663_1">11</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 12" title="Du, R. M., Ma, Z. H., Xie, P. Y., He, Y. &amp; Cen, H. Y. PST: Plant segmentation transformer for 3D point clouds of rapeseed plants at the podding stage. ISPRS J. Photogramm. Remote Sens. 195, 380–392, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1016/j.isprsjprs.2022.11.022&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR12" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e666">12</a></sup>. Consequently, point cloud technology facilitates organ-level phenotypic analysis, allowing for precise calculations of 3D biomass, plant height, volume, and leaf area (as illustrated in Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="table anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Tab1">1</a>). This capability allows researchers to rapidly acquire accurate phenotypic information in large-scale agricultural scenarios. Furthermore, when point cloud data encompasses various growth stages of crops, it enables the dynamic tracking of phenotypic parameters across time. By constructing comprehensive growth models, researchers can effectively track crop growth trends, providing multidimensional data support for breeding, cultivation, and phenotype-genotype correlation analysis.</p><div class="c-article-table" data-test="inline-table" data-container-section="table" id="table-1"><figure><figcaption class="c-article-table__figcaption"><b id="Tab1" data-test="table-caption">Table 1 Specific applications of point cloud data in crop phenotyping research.</b></figcaption><div class="u-text-right u-hide-print"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="table-link" data-track="click" data-track-action="view table" data-track-label="button" rel="nofollow" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0/tables/1" aria-label="Full size table 1"><span>Full size table</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></figure></div><p>Specially customized crop datasets that accurately represent the natural 3D structures of crops are essential for training deep learning models. Several publicly available point clouds focused on plants have been successively emerged, such as Pheno4D<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 13" title="Schunck, D. et al. Pheno4D: A spatio-temporal dataset of maize and tomato plant point clouds for phenotyping and advanced plant analysis. PLoS ONE. 16, e0256340, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0256340&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2021)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR13" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e946">13</a></sup> and Rose-X<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 14" title="Dutagaci, H., Rasti, P., Galopin, G. &amp; Rousseau, D. ROSE-X: an annotated data set for evaluation of 3D plant organ segmentation methods. Plant methods. 16, 1–14, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1186/s13007-020-00573-w&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2020)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR14" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e950">14</a></sup>. Pheno4D comprises point clouds from two crop species, namely maize and tomato, with a total of 126 annotated samples. Rose-X contains only 11 rose point clouds. Furthermore, some unpublished datasets, like the one created by Wang <i>et al</i>.<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 15" title="Wang, L., Zheng, L. &amp; Wang, M.3D point cloud instance segmentation of lettuce based on PartNet. In: Proceedings of the IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition Workshops (CVPRW). PP 1647–1655, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/CVPRW56347.2022.00171&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2022)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR15" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e957">15</a></sup>, concentrate on specific plants such as lettuce. While these datasets are valuable, they often lack diversity in plant species and sample size, rendering them insufficient for broader research tasks.</p><p>It is well-recognized the acquisition of large-scale 3D datasets remains a costly and challenging task<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 16" title="Wu, T. et al. Omniobject3d: Large-vocabulary 3d object dataset for realistic perception, reconstruction and generation. In: Proceedings of the IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR). PP 803–814, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/CVPR52729.2023.00084&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR16" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e965">16</a></sup>, particularly within the agricultural sector. Cultivating crops demands ample space, skilled personnel, and lengthy growth cycles, all of which exacerbate the associated costs. Due to these constraints, some point cloud datasets are derived from artificial designs. Analogous to the methodologies employed by ShapeNet<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 17" title="Chang, A. X. et al. Shapenet: An information-rich 3d model repository. Preprint at &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1512.03012&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2015)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR17" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e969">17</a></sup> and ModelNet40<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 18" title="Wu, Z. et al. 3d shapenets: A deep representation for volumetric shapes. In: Proceedings of the IEEE Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR). PP 1912–1920, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/CVPR.2015.7298801&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2015)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR18" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e973">18</a></sup>, which utilize CAD generated point clouds, Kaya Turgut <i>et al</i>. utilized L-studio software to generate rose point clouds<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 19" title="Turgut, K., Dutagaci, H., Galopin, G. &amp; Rousseau, D. Segmentation of structural parts of rosebush plants with 3D point-based deep learning methods. Plant Methods. 18, 20, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1186/s13007-022-00857-3&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2022)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR19" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e980">19</a></sup>. Although these generated point clouds resemble real objects, they still exhibit discrepancies that limit their applicability in real-world scenarios.</p><p>To address the issue of limited dataset availability, we have meticulously curated the Crops3D point cloud dataset over a span of four years, as illustrated in Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Fig1">1</a>. This comprehensive dataset encompasses a diverse range of eight crop varieties and over a thousand crop point cloud samples. It stands as the pioneering dataset that comprehensively support the three critical tasks in 3D crop phenotyping: instance segmentation of individual plant in agricultural settings, plant type perception, and plant organ segmentation. This dataset holds the potential to significantly advance research and applications in several domains: (a) Exploration of plant instance segmentation algorithms in complex backgrounds. (b) Development of robust classification algorithms suitable for agricultural scenarios. (c) Research on semantic segmentation algorithms for crop organs and phenotypic parameter extraction. (d) Standardized evaluation dataset for crop point cloud classification and segmentation tasks. Additionally, this paper presents the benchmark results of various widely-used deep learning models on the Crops3D dataset.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-1" data-title="Fig. 1"><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig1" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 1</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" srcset="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig1_HTML.png?as=webp"><img aria-describedby="figure-1-desc" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig1_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 1" loading="lazy" width="685" height="425"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0/figures/1" data-track-dest="link:Figure1 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 1" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-1-desc"><p>Crops3D is a diverse crop point cloud dataset, featuring a wealth of 3D crop point clouds sourced from real agricultural scenarios. It is designed to facilitate perception and segmentation tasks related to crop point clouds.</p></div></div></figure></div></div></div></section><section data-title="Methods"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec2-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec2">Methods</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec2-content"><p>In this section, the details of the dataset is explored, elaborating on its composition, the methods employed for data acquisition, the procedures involved in processing, the annotations appended. Statistical results are also furnished for a comprehensive understanding. The dataset is primarily designed to facilitate tasks related to crop classification and segmentation.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec3">Dataset description</h3><p>The dataset is sourced from extensive agricultural environments, and is specifically designed to capture point clouds that represent a wide variety of plants across large cultivation areas. It integrates data from various point cloud acquisition techniques frequently used in agricultural research, ensuring both comprehensiveness and practical applicability. Data collection was conducted on eight crops: cabbage, cotton, maize, potato, rapeseed, rice, tomato, and wheat. Notably, the data for cabbage and tomatoes were collected periodically, enabling continuous tracking of the growth dynamics. The data for maize spans multiple growth stages, including seedling, tassel, and kernel stages. In contrast, data for cotton, potato, rapeseed, rice, and wheat were focused on short-cycle captures within a specific growth period.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec4">Dataset acquisition</h3><p>The dataset is a comprehensive collection, embodying a broad spectrum of acquisition methodologies, each meticulously tailored to cater to the unique requirements of different crops. The methodologies employed span from sensor-based point cloud generation technologies to image-based techniques. In the realm of sensor-based technologies, Terrestrial Laser Scanning (TLS)<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 20" title="Lin, Y. LiDAR: An important tool for next-generation phenotyping technology of high potential for plant phenomics? Comput. Electron. Agric. 119, 61–73, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1016/j.compag.2015.10.011&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2015)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR20" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e1030">20</a></sup> and Structured Light Scanning<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 21" title="Zhang, S. High-speed 3D shape measurement with structured light methods: A review. Opt. Laser Eng. 106, 119–131, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1016/j.optlaseng.2018.02.017&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2018)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR21" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e1034">21</a></sup> are utilized.</p><h4 class="c-article__sub-heading c-article__sub-heading--small" id="Sec5">TLS-based point cloud data collection</h4><p>TLS technology, known for its broad field of view, is exceptionally suited for capturing point clouds in expansive field scenes<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 22" title="Madec, S. et al. High-throughput phenotyping of plant height: comparing unmanned aerial vehicles and ground LiDAR estimates. Front. Plant Sci. 8, 2002, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.3389/fpls.2017.02002&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2017)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR22" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e1045">22</a></sup>. As shown in Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Fig2">2</a>, a TLS device, the FARO Focus S70 (America) was utilized for point cloud data collection across a variety of crops, including maize, cotton, rice, rapeseed, wheat, and potato. This device provides a maximum resolution of 976,000 points per second, enabling rapid and precise data acquisition. The accuracy of TLS results is influenced by factors such as site layout and equipment settings. Important considerations include the distance and angle between the scanner and the target. Excessive distance reduces point cloud density, while insufficient distance might result in data loss. Each plant within the field point cloud data was segmented individually to create a dataset of single-plant point clouds. Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Fig2">2</a> illustrates the acquisition scenarios for six crop species.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-2" data-title="Fig. 2"><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig2" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 2</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" srcset="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig2_HTML.png?as=webp"><img aria-describedby="figure-2-desc" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig2_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 2" loading="lazy" width="685" height="494"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0/figures/2" data-track-dest="link:Figure2 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 2" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-2-desc"><p>TLS-based point cloud generation.</p></div></div></figure></div><h4 class="c-article__sub-heading c-article__sub-heading--small" id="Sec6">Image-based point cloud data generation: tomato</h4><p>In addition to utilizing TLS for field point cloud data acquisition, image-based methods are commonly employed to generate individual plant point clouds. Specifically, tomato images were collected in a greenhouse located in LiaoCheng, Shandong, China from August to October 2020. During the seedling stage, around 100 photos were taken of each tomato plant. As the plants continued to grow, they were divided into upper and lower sections for separate image collection due to occlusion. The Structure from Motion (SfM)<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 23" title="Wang, Y., Hu, S., Ren, H., Yang, W. &amp; Zhai, R. 3DPhenoMVS: A low-cost 3D tomato phenotyping pipeline using 3D reconstruction point cloud based on multiview images. Agronomy. 12, 1865, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.3390/agronomy12081865&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2022)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR23" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e1080">23</a></sup> algorithm was then used to reconstruct the tomatoes in 3D, resulting in a complete 3D point cloud, as shown in Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Fig3">3</a>. For detailed technical information, please refer to the referenced publication, 3DPhenoMVS<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 16" title="Wu, T. et al. Omniobject3d: Large-vocabulary 3d object dataset for realistic perception, reconstruction and generation. In: Proceedings of the IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR). PP 803–814, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/CVPR52729.2023.00084&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR16" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e1087">16</a></sup>. The dataset comprises 83 tomato point cloud samples, encompassing various growth stages of the tomato plants.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-3" data-title="Fig. 3"><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig3" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 3</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" srcset="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig3_HTML.png?as=webp"><img aria-describedby="figure-3-desc" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig3_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 3" loading="lazy" width="685" height="292"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0/figures/3" data-track-dest="link:Figure3 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 3" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-3-desc"><p>Image-based point cloud generation for tomato plants.</p></div></div></figure></div><h4 class="c-article__sub-heading c-article__sub-heading--small" id="Sec7">Cabbage point cloud generation using both technologies</h4><p>In this study, laser scanning and image-based methods were employed to generate point cloud data for purple cabbage plants. The active data collection involved the use of a structured light scanner, while the image-based approach utilized an enhanced CasMVSNet, a deep learning-based network, for 3D reconstruction. Data from 14 purple cabbage plants in a natural setting were collected weekly from September 1 to December 5, 2022, using both RGB images and structured light data. Images were captured with a Canon 77D camera at a resolution of 6,000 × 4,000 pixels, while a Wiiboox Reeyee-Pro scanner provided 3D point cloud data. Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Fig4">4</a> illustrates the acquisition methods, showing RGB data collected in 360 degrees at varying heights. During the seedling stage, 60–80 images were taken at two heights, increasing to 100–120 as plants grew. An improved CasMVSNet was employed to generate 3D structures from these images<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 24" title="Ren, H. et al. Three-Dimensional Plant Reconstruction with Enhanced Cascade-MVSNet. In: Proceedings of the 6th Chinese Conference on Pattern Recognition and Computer Vision (PRCV). PP 283–294, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1007/978-981-99-8432-9_23&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR24" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e1119">24</a></sup>, while the scanner synthesized structured light data to generate point clouds.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-4" data-title="Fig. 4"><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig4" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 4</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" srcset="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig4_HTML.png?as=webp"><img aria-describedby="figure-4-desc" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig4_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 4" loading="lazy" width="685" height="522"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0/figures/4" data-track-dest="link:Figure4 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 4" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-4-desc"><p>Purple cabbage data collection.</p></div></div></figure></div><h4 class="c-article__sub-heading c-article__sub-heading--small" id="Sec8">Comparison of acquisition methods</h4><p>It is noteworthy that each point cloud acquisition method possesses specific advantages and limitations. TLS is particularly suited for large agricultural crops and extensive environments due to its exceptional range and precision. However, its high cost presents a significant drawback. Structured light scanning, on the other hand, is highly effective in obtaining high-quality crop point clouds but is limited by a narrow field of view and sensitivity to lighting conditions. Therefore, it is more suitable for smaller crops and controlled indoor environments. Image-based point cloud generation is renowned for its cost-effectiveness, but it necessitates complex data processing algorithms, such as the Multi-View Stereo (MVS) deep learning approach CasMVSNet<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 25" title="Gu, X. et al. Cascade cost volume for high-resolution multi-view stereo and stereo matching. In: Proceedings of the IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR). PP 2495–2504, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/CVPR42600.2020.00257&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2020)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR25" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e1149">25</a></sup>.</p><p>Acquiring complete and accurate point cloud data is essential for the precise extraction of phenotypic parameters. Missing significant portions of point cloud data can introduce errors in identification and segmentation, thereby complicating subsequent phenotypic analyses. Therefore, it is crucial to consider the morphological and structural characteristics of the crops when selecting an appropriate acquisition method. For example, crops with elongated leaves such as rice and wheat lack distinct features in images, rendering image-based methods less effective. In such cases, sensor-based approaches like TLS can yield superior point cloud data. Conversely, for crops with broad leaves and rich features, such as cabbage, TLS, structured light, and image-based methods are all viable options. Notably, mutual occlusion and self-occlusion remain pervasive challenges that none of these methods can fully address.</p><h4 class="c-article__sub-heading c-article__sub-heading--small" id="Sec9">Data annotation</h4><p>To facilitate the use of this dataset by researchers, the organs of all crop samples were annotated in this study. CloudCompare (version 2.12.4) software was employed to annotate the point cloud. This software is a powerful open-source point cloud processing tool that provides various point cloud processing operators, such as filtering and denoising. It also supports various editing and cropping operations, providing convenience for point cloud annotation processing. When performing semantic annotation of plant point clouds, common practices include using different colors to mark or adding scalar field (SF) attributes to the point cloud. However, modifying the point cloud color may overwrite the original color information, so in most cases, adding a new attribute column to the point cloud data has become a more commonly used semantic annotation method.</p><p>Using the maize point cloud as a case study, statistical filtering was applied to the individual plant point cloud to remove any outliers. CloudCompare was then utilized to segregate the point cloud into six distinct semantic segments: soil, pot container, stem, leaves, tassel, and fruit. Each segment was allocated a unique scalar field (SF) attribute value. Following this segmentation, these annotated point clouds were combined to create a comprehensive annotated point cloud dataset, as illustrated in Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Fig5">5</a>. Within the plot point clouds, the data was primarily divided into ground and plant sections. The SF for the ground section was labeled as 0, while the plant sections were labeled from 1 to n to distinguish different plant objects, where n represents the total number of distinct maize plants in the plot.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-5" data-title="Fig. 5"><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig5" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 5</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" srcset="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig5_HTML.png?as=webp"><img aria-describedby="figure-5-desc" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig5_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 5" loading="lazy" width="685" height="456"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0/figures/5" data-track-dest="link:Figure5 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 5" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-5-desc"><p>Annotation of maize point cloud.</p></div></div></figure></div><p>Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="table anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Tab2">2</a> provides a detailed overview of our dataset, highlighting the unique acquisition techniques employed for point clouds of various crops, along with the total number of point clouds and the classification post-annotation. Specialized methods were adopted for collecting point clouds for each crop type, focusing on capturing their unique features and structures. This methodical strategy guarantees a thorough depiction of diverse plant structures in the dataset, facilitating detailed studies in various agricultural sectors.</p><div class="c-article-table" data-test="inline-table" data-container-section="table" id="table-2"><figure><figcaption class="c-article-table__figcaption"><b id="Tab2" data-test="table-caption">Table 2 Dataset details for various crops.</b></figcaption><div class="u-text-right u-hide-print"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="table-link" data-track="click" data-track-action="view table" data-track-label="button" rel="nofollow" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0/tables/2" aria-label="Full size table 2"><span>Full size table</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></figure></div><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec10">Dataset preparation</h3><p>Crop point cloud were carefully selected from acquired scenes and refined through techniques such as cropping and denoising. Various parts of the crop point cloud were then annotated for detailed analysis. Currently, many point cloud deep learning models necessitate a consistent number of points across the point clouds. However, the natural variability in plant sizes leads to differing point counts, posing a significant challenge. To address this, it’s essential to subsample the point clouds to ensure a uniform number of points across the dataset. Farthest Point Sampling (FPS) is a widely used method for sampling point clouds, and it allows for uniform sampling across the point cloud while preserving geometric details. Considering the structural diversity in plant stems and leaves, conventional sampling points such as 2,048 or 4,096 may result in the loss of significant details. To retain more crop features, FPS was utilized to sample the original point cloud to 10,000 points, thereby constructing the Crops3D_10k dataset. Additionally, instance segmentation annotations were conducted on plant cultivation plots obtained via TLS, resulting in the Crops3D_IS dataset.</p><h4 class="c-article__sub-heading c-article__sub-heading--small" id="Sec11">Corrupt dataset</h4><p>In light of the unique challenges of data collection in agricultural environments, where noise and missing data are prevalent, the ability of models to accurately perceive crops is of significant importance in agricultural practice. To comprehensively test the performance of model classification, we construct a corruption dataset named Crops3D_10k-C, derived from Crops3D_10k using methods from modlenet-C<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 26" title="Ren, J., Pan, L. &amp; Liu, Z.Benchmarking and analyzing point cloud classification under corruptions. In: Proceedings of the International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML). PP 18559–18575, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2202.03377&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2022)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR26" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e1582">26</a></sup>. As illustrated in Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Fig6">6</a>, this dataset comprises seven types of corrupted point clouds. This dataset specifically tests the robustness of classification models against various abnormal data challenges. Therefore, we provide four subdivisions of the dataset: Crops3D, subsampled Crops3D_10k, Crops3D_10k-C for evaluating classification robustness, and Crops3D_IS for instance segmentation.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-6" data-title="Fig. 6"><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig6" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 6</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" srcset="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig6_HTML.png?as=webp"><img aria-describedby="figure-6-desc" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig6_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 6" loading="lazy" width="685" height="370"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0/figures/6" data-track-dest="link:Figure6 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 6" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-6-desc"><p>Clean point clouds and corrupt point clouds constructed with seven different corruptions.</p></div></div></figure></div><p>The Crops3D_10k-C dataset is comprised of seven types of corruptions, designed to simulate various real-world phenomena that may affect point cloud data. These corruptions include:</p>
                    <h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="FPar1">Jitter</h3>
                    <p>Implemented by applying small, random displacements to the positions of points within the point cloud, this corruption simulates the effect of sensor jitter or random errors during data collection. The displacements follow a Gaussian distribution with mean <span class="mathjax-tex">\(\mu =0\)</span> and standard deviation <span class="mathjax-tex">\({\rm{\sigma }}=10\times {\rm{level}}\)</span>.</p>
                  
                    <h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="FPar2">Rotation and scale</h3>
                    <p>These transformations simulate the effects of rotational movements due to changes in object or sensor positioning, and dimensional changes due to crop growth, respectively. Rotation is performed randomly around the XYZ axes, with the degree of rotation increasing with the level, up to a maximum of <i>π</i>/6. Scaling involves random dimensional adjustments in the xyz axes, controlled by a scaling factor s.</p>
                  
                    <h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="FPar3">Global point addition (Add<sub>G</sub>) and local point addition (Add<sub>L</sub>)</h3>
                    <p>These methods involve adding points either globally across the entire point cloud or locally within specific regions. This simulates sensor noise or anomalies during data acquisition by randomly interspersing additional points. <span class="mathjax-tex">\({\rm{Ad}}{{\rm{d}}}_{{\rm{G}}}\)</span> generates <span class="mathjax-tex">\(10\times {\rm{level}}\)</span> new points within a unit sphere centered at the centroid of the point cloud, integrating these points with the original point cloud. <span class="mathjax-tex">\({\rm{Ad}}{{\rm{d}}}_{{\rm{L}}}\)</span> randomly selects a seed point from the point cloud and generating <span class="mathjax-tex">\(10\times {\rm{level}}\)</span> new points around it. These points follow a Gaussian distribution, with the standard deviation (<i>σ</i>) being randomly chosen from a uniform distribution <i>U</i>(0.075,0.125).</p>
                  
                    <h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="FPar4">Drop global point (Drop<sub>G</sub>) and drop local point (Drop<sub>L</sub>)</h3>
                    <p>These techniques model data loss by randomly removing points across the entire point cloud or within specified local areas. For Drop<sub>G</sub>, a percentage of points defined by the severity level is removed, whereas for Drop<sub>L</sub>, the <span class="mathjax-tex">\(100\times {\rm{level}}\)</span> nearest points to a selected seed point are removed.</p>
                    <p>Each of the seven corruptions is applied at five different severity levels, providing a graded approach to studying the impact of these perturbations on subsequent point cloud processing tasks. The specific parameters and methodologies for each level are detailed in Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="table anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Tab3">3</a>.</p><div class="c-article-table" data-test="inline-table" data-container-section="table" id="table-3"><figure><figcaption class="c-article-table__figcaption"><b id="Tab3" data-test="table-caption">Table 3 Corruption definition.</b></figcaption><div class="u-text-right u-hide-print"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="table-link" data-track="click" data-track-action="view table" data-track-label="button" rel="nofollow" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0/tables/3" aria-label="Full size table 3"><span>Full size table</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></figure></div>
                  </div></div></section><section data-title="Data Records"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec12-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec12">Data Records</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec12-content"><p>The dataset is stored in the figshare database and consists of a series of PLY and HDF5 files<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 27" title="Zhu, J. et al. Crops3D a diverse 3D crop dataset for realistic perception and segmentation toward agricultural applications. Figshare &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.27313272&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2024)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR27" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e2586">27</a></sup>. The data is stored and compressed into a file named Crops3D.zip, which includes four directories: Crops3D, Crops3D_10k, Crops3D_100k-C, and Crops3D_IS.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec13">Crops3D directory</h3><p>The Crops3D directory contains annotated raw point cloud data. It includes eight directories and two text files. The eight directories are named Cabbage, Cotton, Maize, Potato, Rapeseed, Rice, Tomato, and Wheat, each storing PLY point cloud files corresponding to the respective crop. The two text files list the relative paths of samples in the training and test sets.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec14">Crops3D_10k directory</h3><p>The Crops3D_10k directory contains point cloud data subsampled to 10,000 points using FPS. The file structure mirrors that of Crops3D, ensuring consistency and ease of use.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec15">Crops3D_10k-C directory</h3><p>The Crops3D_IS directory contains a corrupt directory and two HDF5 (H5) files representing the training and test datasets. The corrupt directory contains 36 H5 files: one clean test set and 35 files corresponding to seven types of corrupted test sets, each divided into five severity levels.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec16">Crops3D_IS directory</h3><p>The Crops3D_IS directory contains point cloud data intended for individual plant segmentation at the plot scale, known as instance segmentation. It contains one subdirectory and two text files. This subdirectory stores point cloud data for 50 plots, covering maize, potato, and rapeseed crops, formatted in PLY. The two text files enumerate the relative paths of samples in the training and test sets.</p></div></div></section><section data-title="Technical Validation"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec17-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec17">Technical Validation</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec17-content"><p>The annotation quality of the dataset was validated by evaluating the consistency between the labeled data and annotations from multiple experts (E1, E2, E3, E4, E5, E6, E7). For comparison, sixteen point cloud samples were randomly selected from the dataset and provided to seven experts for manual segmentation. Subsequently, the mean Intersection over Union (mIoU) and Accuracy (Acc) between the dataset ground truth (GT) and the different expert annotations were calculated. Additionally, the Intraclass Correlation Coefficient (ICC) and Cohen’s Kappa were used to indicate the level of agreement between the GT and expert annotations.</p><p>Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Fig7">7(a,b)</a> illustrate the mIoU and Acc of the dataset’s GT compared to annotations by various experts. The results demonstrate a strong consistency between the dataset GT and the expert annotations, with overall mIoU exceeding 0.91 and Acc surpassing 0.97, indicating high annotation accuracy. Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Fig7">7(c,d</a>) present the ICC and Kappa values between the dataset GT and the seven expert annotations. For the sixteen samples analyzed, the overall ICC exceeded 0.98, and Kappa exceeded 0.97. The lowest ICC for an individual sample was 0.85, and the lowest Kappa was 0.80, further confirming a high level of consistency between the dataset GT and the expert annotations.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-7" data-title="Fig. 7"><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig7" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 7</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" srcset="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig7_HTML.png?as=webp"><img aria-describedby="figure-7-desc" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41597-024-04290-0/MediaObjects/41597_2024_4290_Fig7_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 7" loading="lazy" width="685" height="427"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0/figures/7" data-track-dest="link:Figure7 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 7" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-7-desc"><p>Segmentation verification of the Crops3D dataset.</p></div></div></figure></div><p>To validate the utility of the dataset, a comprehensive evaluation was presented for a carefully curated selection of widely used models in the field. This evaluation measures their performance on crop point clouds, covering both classification and segmentation tasks using the Crops3D dataset. Compared to publicly available datasets for other 3D objects, Crops3D presents unique characteristics, including a diverse range of crop species and an extended growth coverage. These distinctive qualities set Crops3D dataset apart, offering a challenging and expansive repository for conducting point cloud classification and segmentation tasks within the realm of agricultural applications. This comprehensive evaluation will provide valuable insights for point cloud processing in agriculture, assisting researchers and practitioners in making informed decisions when selecting suitable models.</p><p>The experiments were conducted using a server equipped with four NVIDIA V100 32 G GPUs. Throughout the entire experimental process, we maintained the original hyperparameter settings and adhered to uniform evaluation criteria. This approach was adopted to ensure the consistency and comparability of results across the entire experimental process.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec18">Robust perception</h3><h4 class="c-article__sub-heading c-article__sub-heading--small" id="Sec19">Evaluating indicator</h4><p>Object-level point cloud classification is one of the fundamental tasks in point cloud perception, with the robustness of classification primarily depending on architecture design, pre-training and data augmentation methods. In this section, we evaluate the performance of multiple models on the classification task using the Crops3D dataset. We primarily leverage the corruption dataset, Crops3D_10k-C, introduced in Methods Section, to assess the classification robustness of various models on crop corruption data without pre-training and data augmentation. The specific formula for computing mean Corruption Error (mCE) is as follows (Eq. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="equation anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Equ2">2</a>), primarily using the performance metrics of DGCNN<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 28" title="Wang, Y. et al. Dynamic graph cnn for learning on point clouds. ACM Trans. Graphics. 38, 1–12, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1145/3326362&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2019)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR28" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e2678">28</a></sup> as the baseline to normalize the error rates for various corruption types (Eq. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="equation anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Equ1">1</a>).</p><div id="Equ1" class="c-article-equation"><div class="c-article-equation__content"><span class="mathjax-tex">$${{\rm{CE}}}_{i}=\frac{\mathop{\sum }\limits_{l=1}^{5}\left(1-{{\rm{OA}}}_{i,l}\right)}{\mathop{\sum }\limits_{l=1}^{5}\left(1-{{\rm{OA}}}_{i,l}^{{\rm{DGCNN}}}\right)}$$</span></div><div class="c-article-equation__number">
                    (1)
                </div></div><div id="Equ2" class="c-article-equation"><div class="c-article-equation__content"><span class="mathjax-tex">$$\text{mCE}\,=\,\frac{1}{{\rm{N}}}\mathop{\sum }\limits_{i=1}^{{\rm{N}}}{\text{CE}}_{i}$$</span></div><div class="c-article-equation__number">
                    (2)
                </div></div><p>Here, <i>i</i> represents various types of corruptions, including adding points, removing points, jittering, rotating, and scaling. <i>l</i>, ranging from small to large, denotes the severity of the perturbation. <span class="mathjax-tex">\({{\rm{OA}}}_{i,l}\)</span> denotes the Overall Accuracy of the model on the <i>i</i>-th type of corruption at severity level <i>l</i>, and <span class="mathjax-tex">\({{\rm{OA}}}_{i,l}^{{\rm{DGCNN}}}\)</span> indicates the Overall Accuracy of the baseline model DGCNN for the same corruption type and level. N represents the number of corrupt settings.</p><p>A lower mCE signifies robustness of the model to corruption data, suggesting that the model is better equipped to handle variations and maintain accuracy across corrupted datasets. This evaluation method provides insights into each model’s generalization capability in realistic scenarios where data corruption is prevalent.</p><h4 class="c-article__sub-heading c-article__sub-heading--small" id="Sec20">Model comparison</h4><p>Nine widely adopted point cloud classification models, namely DGCNN, PointNet<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 29" title="Qi, C. R., Su, H., Mo, K. &amp; Guibas, L. J.Pointnet: Deep learning on point sets for 3d classification and segmentation. In: Proceedings of the IEEE Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR). PP 652–660, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1109/CVPR.2017.16&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2017)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR29" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e3009">29</a></sup>, PointNet++<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 30" title="Qi, C. R., Yi, L., Su, H. &amp; Guibas, L. J.Pointnet++: Deep hierarchical feature learning on point sets in a metric space. In: Proceedings of the 31st International Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NIPS). PP 5105–5114, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1706.02413&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2017)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR30" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e3013">30</a></sup>, PointCNN<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 31" title="Li, Y. et al. Pointcnn: Convolution on x-transformed points. In: Proceedings of the 32nd Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NIPS). PP 828–838, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1801.07791&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2018)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR31" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e3017">31</a></sup>, CurveNet<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 32" title="Xiang, T., Zhang, C., Song, Y., Yu, J. &amp; Cai, W.Walk in the cloud: Learning curves for point clouds shape analysis. In: Proceedings of the IEEE/CVF International Conference on Computer Vision (ICCV). PP 915–924, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.27313272i.org/10.1109/ICCV48922.2021.00095&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2021)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR32" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e3021">32</a></sup>, Point Cloud Transformer (PCT)<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 33" title="Guo, M.-H. et al. Pct: Point cloud transformer. Comput. Vis. Media. 7, 187–199, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1007/s41095-021-0229-5&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2021)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR33" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e3025">33</a></sup>, PointMLP<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 34" title="Ma, X., Qin, C., You, H., Ran, H. &amp; Fu, Y.Rethinking network design and local geometry in point cloud: A simple residual MLP framework. In: Proceedings of the International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR). PP 1–15, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2202.07123&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2022)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR34" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e3030">34</a></sup>, PointMamba<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 35" title="Liang, D. et al. PointMamba: A Simple State Space Model for Point Cloud Analysis. Preprint at &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2202.07123&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2024)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR35" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e3034">35</a></sup> and DeLA<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 36" title="Yang, W. et al. DeLA: An extremely faster network with decoupled local aggregation for large scale point cloud learning. Int. J. Appl. Earth Obs. Geoinf. 135, 104255, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jag.2024.104255&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2024)." href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#ref-CR36" id="ref-link-section-d399225442e3038">36</a></sup>, were trained on the crops3D_10k training set and conducted a comprehensive evaluation on clean (cln) and corrupted (corr) test sets, as shown in Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="table anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Tab4">4</a>. The models were trained on Crops3D_10k and evaluated for robustness on Crops3D_10k-C, where a lower mCE value signified better classification robustness of the network. On the clean test set, all models accurately classified crop point clouds. However, on various corrupted datasets, the classification performance of the models showed varying degrees of decline, as illustrated in Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41597-024-04290-0#Fig8">8</a>. The horizontal axis denotes various models, while the vertical axis represents OA. Each corruption is distinguished by a distinct color.</p><div class="c-article-table" data-test="inline-table" data-container-section="table" id="table-4"><figure><figcaption class="c-article-table__figcaption"><b id="Tab4" data-test="table-caption">Table 4 Robustness results of point cloud perception on Crops3D across various architectural designs.</b></figcaption><div
